For the chapter's cast, the announcement is both consolation and correction. Consolation: Mary, the exhiled believers of Abyssinia, the anxious community around the Prophet — all are told that the earth is not held in perpetuity by those who appear to hold it. Correction: the 'wealth and children as adornment' psychology of the Arabs, whom the sūrah will shortly rebuke (19:74–80), is pensioned off beforehand. Property, parties, and prestige are temporary occupancies, not acquisitions: every earth-file will be seen to belong, in its final folder, to God's archive.

**Return as the Other Half of the Same Sentence**

*And to Us they will all be returned* completes the equation: inheritance of the world entails the gathering of its inhabitants. The two halves of the verse imitate, and are, the jaws of the divine recovery — the material globe repossessed on one side, the conscious parties repossessed on the other. They are not parallel actions: the return of people is the necessary sequel of the inheritance of the earth, since no one is left any place to go.

*Rajʿ* — return — is also the doctrinal term inside which the chapter's whole eschatological movement has been traveling: the day of regret foreseen (19:39), the sharpened hearing and eyesight measured out (19:38), are waypoints of this single homebound journey of the human race. For the chapter's justice theme, meanwhile, the return tells the Muslim of Makkah desertion and abuse that his oppressors are not headed anywhere different from where he is headed: one spot, one docket, one Heir before whom titles are read. The wronged and the wronging are both in the moving column; only the Judge is stationary.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Pivot**

Sūrah Maryam has just spent forty verses presenting God's mercy in case files — His answers to prayer, His investments in weak and hidden people. Verse 40 closes the case against the other half of the evidence: the world the merciful acts took place in is temporary collateral. Both halves belong to one doctrine of aid: the gifts of God pointed forward, not back; no mercy of His is an earthly reprieve written forever.

With ownership and return established, the sūrah will now resume its gallery — a father-son confrontation, a fugitive's migration, the patriarchs of Arabia's own descent — but everything in them now reads as characters inside the verse's law. Abraham argues inside inherited earth; Moses receives scripture under a tenancy; Ishmael waits out appointments on leased time; and over them all, and over us, sole and terminal, stands the Inheritor, whose return-call is the one itinerary every creature holds in advance.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:41

> And mention in the Book ˹O Prophet, the story of˺ Abraham. He was surely a man of truth and a prophet

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mention: The Roll Call Resumes**

The command to *mention* — which opened Mary's file at verse 16 — returns here to open the second great gallery of the chapter: the patriarchs. The Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ is again addressed first; he is to *recall* Abraham, with all the communal re-contextualization that act implies. In Makkah, to speak of Abraham was not to introduce a foreign name but to touch the city's own founding devotion: Quraysh named him father of their line, keeper of their sanctuary's memory, the patriarch of their pilgrimage. The command reaches into the audience's proudest memory and prepares to turn it.

The formula also links structurally back: Mary was a daughter to be *mentioned*; Abraham is now the father. And in a sūrah whose recurring plot is God's care for those separated from their kin, the choice of the next story is direct: the son who had to oppose his own father, the heaven-born man who walked out of his household with nothing but invocation — his entry into the chapter continues the theme the verse of inheritance (19:40) just enunciated; every earthly belonging will revert to God, so the prophet models leaving before the reversion.

**Ṣiddīq: Truthfulness as a Title of Rank**

Abraham is styled with a title that, in the Qur'an, is a rank: *ṣiddīq* — the man of truth, whose truthfulness is instinct, habit, and constitution — the title given also to Joseph (12:46), to Idrīs in this chapter (19:56), and to Mary: “His mother was a woman of truth” (5:75). Al-Rāzī unpacks the quality into nature and practice: honest in grain and honest in deed — truthful in his speech, his promises, his undertakings (al-Ṭabarī) — and withal one who *confirms* truth wherever it confronts him: the ṣiddīq does not merely not-lie; he attests to God.

The Book itself enrolls the ṣiddīqūn in the highest roll of humanity: “And whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be in the company of those blessed by Allah: the prophets, the people of truth, the martyrs, and the righteous—what honourable company!” (4:69), and elsewhere, “it is they who are ˹truly˺ the people of truth” (57:19) — the word stands second only to 'prophet.' For the sūrah's audience, the application is immediate: Makkah had to choose between tribal honor and honest recognition. To be Abraham's children is not a blood-type; it is to narrate truth as he did, even when it dislodges the family gods.

**Truthful Across Three Registers**

The commentators note that Abraham's ṣidq ran through his speech, his promises, and his acts (al-Ṭabarī) — a completeness measurable across the Qur'an's own life-record of him. Speech: his arguments are the Book's model daʿwah, plain and courteous (6:74–83). Promise: his pledge to his father to pray for him is kept until God defines its limit (9:114); his prayers for his descendants are fulfilled by God for millennia (14:37; 26:84). Deed: his life is sacrifice catalogued — his father, his people, his homeland, his son when the dream commanded, only refrained by God's own substitution (37:103–107).

The whole that emerges from the registers is one being integrated by truthfulness into single-mindedness: “Indeed, I have turned my face ˹toward Allah˺...” (6:162; by association 6:79 — cited elsewhere in the Book: his turning exclusive). For the chapter's deeper weave, ṣiddīq also anticipates the sūrah's finale at Mary's report — “When she delivered...” (3:36) — where God is fully knowing of what anyone delivers; Abraham, like Mary, is shown *delivering* truth without discount in a climate of lies, and God delivering them both.

**Prophet and Messenger: A Verse for Precision**

The title-pairing — truthful, *and a prophet* — occasioned classical attention since, in developed theology, the offices part: *nabī* is anyone commissioned with God's message to convey, of warning or good news; *rasūl*, titled messenger, brings a constitutive scripture or law. Abraham, uniquely among the chapter's prophetic subjects, receives only *nabī* here — and the source-annotated discussion explains why nothing of his vaunted rank is thereby lost: messengership in his case is undoubted (he was given scriptures: “the Scriptures of Abraham and Moses” (87:19); see also 53:36–37) — only the Book's customary precision is at work, reserving *rasūl* this passage for Moss and Ishmael (19:51, 19:54).

For the chapter's argument the title's presence, not its refinement, carries the point: Abraham stands under God's own attestation as commissioned truth-teller before his story even unfolds — so that what follows in verses 42–47 is read, not as a son's rebellion (the old tribal charge), but as the discharge of office. The Book's habit of ordaining his rank first, then narrating, lends the ensuing dialogue its proper juridical frame: the youth speaks with institutional warrant; the father with only elder's habitude.

**The Chapter's Strategic Son**

Why Abraham, why now? The chapter is building its proof of mercy's constancy through a family album: John to Zachariah, Jesus to Mary — and now the sons of God's generosity to Abraham (19:49–50) — the chain hinging on a prophet who had stood childless before his people. But the Abraham file adds what the others could not: a conflict marked *filial*. The sūrah will have the father rebuffed with unrelenting gentleness (19:42–47), the believer's model kin-relation under trial, the consummate politeness of monotheism's son.

For the Makkans, the juxtaposition is surgical: their Qurayshi identity fused lineage, idol-keeping, and pride of place into one substance; Abraham's file is God's solvency on just that substance. The son who proceeds against his father's polytheism is not a stranger brought in to judge their ways; he is the ancestor in whose name they keep the Kaaba. Mentioned in God's Book and certified truthful and prophet — the one whose voice is about to fill the next verses with *dear father* while rejecting his father's gods.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:42

> ˹Remember˺ when he said to his father, “O dear father! Why do you worship what can neither hear nor see, nor benefit you at all

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Dialogue Opens in the Household**

The scene begins without transition: a son addresses his father about theology. The commentators place the addressee with precision: Abraham's father, named Āzar in the Qur'an's later account, “when Abraham said to his father, Ȃzar, ‘Do you take idols as gods? It is clear to me that you and your people are entirely misguided’” (6:74). The confrontation belongs to a series across the Book (6:76–83; 21:51–67; 26:69–87; 37:84–96; 43:26–28), and the sūrah's version is distinguished by its gentleness — reasoned petitions under the vocative *yā abati*, dear father — and by what it chooses first: a question, not an accusation.

That the engagement happens at home is doctrinally weighted. Prophetic confrontation with idolatry in the Qur'an normally occurs across the public square; this one is measured at the hearth, where the cost of speech is maximal and the temptation to peace maximal. The chapter has been praising withdrawal-from-kin as mercy's instrument for forty verses; Abraham's version of the withdrawal begins, rather, in conversation — before leaving the house, he speaks in it. The sūrah thereby places the believer's hardest freedom, the freedom to disagree at home, at the center of its prophet-file.

**The Triple Disability of the Worshipped**

The son's first move is an anatomy of his father's gods, counted on one hand: what you worship *cannot hear, cannot see, cannot benefit you in anything.* The rhetorical form deserves study: the verse does not begin by asserting one God but begins by subtracting faculties from many gods. Al-Rāzī gives the subtraction its logical development: what neither hears nor sees cannot distinguish a worshipper from a passerby, nor receive an invocation, much less answer one — and what cannot benefit its votary *in anything* commands no rational claim to devotion at all; worship then becomes a transaction with the lesser, the worshipper stooping below his own level of agency to honor what is beneath him.

Qur'anic pedagogy consistently provides the argument's confirmation. Abraham deploys its exact form in the square: “He rebuked ˹them˺, ‘Do you then worship—instead of Allah—what can neither benefit nor harm you in any way?’” (21:66), and the whole assembly, pressed, can answer only “Or can they benefit or harm you?” (26:73). The Book's theology of the God who *hears* and *sees* (4:134 et al.) is the silent premise everywhere: worship's minimal requirement is an addressee with attributes; a thing that hears and sees nothing fails before the question of One or Many has even been proposed.

**What the Idolaters Actually Thought**

Al-Rāzī adds a paragraph of intellectual history that dignifies the debate: the worshippers of sculpted figures, he notes, seldom thought the stone itself conscious. The idols functioned as representatives — of stars, to which some of Abraham's milieu attributed effective power; or of great departed men, whose intercession was sought with God. Idol-worship, in this reconstruction, is a protocol of access, not an adoration of matter.

The Qur'anic argument is framed to work even against this sophisticated version. Whatever mediating rationale is given — symbolism, intercession, tutelary presence — the faculties in question still fail: stones cannot broker access to anything they cannot perceive, and dead intercessors can only wait upon God's own decree (2:255's premise). Abraham's three subtractions leave no updating of paganism untouched: if you say the block is only a focus, then surely you are invoking what is beyond it — and what is beyond it is already hearing you without the block (35:14). His argument, in short, shows that idolatry is a contradiction even on its own most charitable account.

**Dear Father: Piety in the Vocative**

The argument's means of delivery is itself half of its force: each salvo across this sequence — verses 42, 43, 44, 45 — is fronted with *yā abati*, an affectionate vocative preserved in every translation's tone: **O dear father**. The commentators (al-Rāzī) read the choice as deliberate: critique of substance married to filial gentleness, and the repetition of the endearment functioning as evidence that the goal is rescue rather than victory. Nothing of address like shaming, jeering, or commanding passes the son's lips, although the moment warranted the strongest possible critique in religious terms.

The contrast carries forward into the sūrah's Makkan hearing as unspoken exhortation: the Prophet's community lived inside polytheist households, torn between kinship and confession, and the verse distributes a curriculum: tell truth, decline worship, and call your father *dear father* while doing it. Emotional gentleness in Abraham is not weakness preparing to capitulate; in verse 46's answer, the father explodes with threat, yet the tenderness of the son's vocative never retaliates, and the tenderness is part of the proof of the rank declared in verse 41: this is how the certified truthful conduct their disputes.

**The Form of the Question as Respect**

Notice the interrogative form: *Why do you worship...* The son does not open with *my* counter-claim; he asks for their ground. Classical muezzins of diction see in it a two-fold respect: a question acknowledges the other's agency — giving the father room to state a rational basis — while, in asking, it already plants the interrogated consciousness before its own deficit. Good daʿwah, the verse instructs, must sometimes be issued as question rather than verdict; a question is a door that lets its addressee walk himself out.

The whole of verses 42–45 descends from that opening interrogation: after the question of reason (42) comes the offer of guidance (43), the exposure of the real director behind the idols (44), and the fearful warning of consequence for the father himself (45). The son begins with the gentlest approach that monotheism owns: look at your gods; count their capacities; tell me why.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:43

> O dear father! I have certainly received some knowledge which you have not received, so follow me and I will guide you to the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge Arrives Out of Order**

The son's second address states, in the plainest possible social inversion, the ranking claim of revelation: *I have certainly received some knowledge which you have not received.* A young man in a tribal household — ranked by birth-year and obedience — asserts epistemic seniority over the elder. The commentators are right to note the shock registered (al-Qurṭubī): the argument depends for its legitimacy *only* on the source — knowledge descended from God, *ʿilm* of Himself and the Hereafter, which the elder, with all his years, could not have gathered from the square or the stars. Authority is relocated, for one sentence, from age to revelation.

The placement of the verse, second in the salvo of five, is exquisitely ordered: verse 42 demolished the false object; now verse 43 builds the alternative — the son as teacher. There is no protective sugar-coating, no diplomatic false-modesty in his words, and egalitarian rhetoric of the kind 'we are both ignorant before the question' does not appear either. The verse says flatly what prophecy must sometimes say: my knowledge from heaven outranks your ignorance from tradition, and good fatherhood — even good elderhood — may mean taking instruction from those younger in the flesh.

**'So Follow Me': The Inverted Command**

The conclusion the son draws is the verse's verb of command: *fattabiʿnī*, follow me. In the tribal household's grammar, following flows from son upward to father; this clause reverses the current, guided not by blood seniorship but by what has been *received*. The commentators (al-Qurṭubī) underscore the bearing for all audiences who had ever hidden behind heritage: if a father may be bid by a son to *follow because the son has received guidance*, then lineage loses its veto over creed. The verse is among the sūrah's quiet landmines below the Makkan discourse of ancestry.

The Quraysh counter-position is named in God's book almost in Abraham's own idiom: “When it is said to them, ‘Follow what Allah has revealed,’ they reply, ‘No! We ˹only˺ follow what we found our forefathers practicing’” (2:170). Abraham's story is heaven's refutation of that reflex before it is stated as reflex: the ancestor whom Makkah claims as forefather is here commanding *his own father* to follow him — and the father's obstinate following-of-tradition is the cautionary half of the model. Following, in God's grammar, follows the route of revealed knowledge, not the trail of seniority.

**The Humble Superiority of 'I Will Guide You'**

The offer completes and mollifies the correction: *I will guide you to the Straight Path.* Note the modesty carried inside the promise — he does not say 'I will show that I am right'; he promises a destination, *and* he promises it to the same elder he has just outranked. The gentle form the superiority takes is service: the son's revealed knowledge would rescue the father's destination. The guidance is not held as prestige but offered as filial duty of a new kind.

Within the chapter's repeated concepts, the offer also resonates with the entire sūrah's mercy-print: God 'extending' through the prophet what the father lacks (compare His bestowals of children, as on Zachariah (19:7), or Mary's provision (19:24–25)). Guidance is distributed as one of God’s mercies via human channel, and the chapter-school of service to kin (19:14, 19:32) is seated alongside the demand for truth: the prophet's superior knowledge exists to be spent on those who do not have it, and Abraham's file makes the first beneficiary of his gift the very man he could, in honor-cultures, have simply left behind in error.

**A Path Called Straight in the Midst of a Crooked Household**

The destination's name carries the Book's unvarying cartography: the *Straight Path* (ṣirāṭ mustaqīm; the phrase recurs within the chapter at 19:36, where Jesus settles it in the same designation). Commentators gloss it clinically (al-Qurṭubī): the way of correct religion on which salvation is attainable — untwisted by either error or turns, unstarred by idolatrous detours. Abraham does not offer his father a sect but a line: flat, direct, conceptually minimal, same as it was to Adam, same as it was for Jesus later in the chapter.

The geometry is also a precise answer to what verse 42 had already implied: idols bend — in their multiplicity, in the ever-revised hierarchies assigned them, in the tribal compromises by which a man prays to his father's stones because they are his father's. A single God addressed directly is, by contrast, the only *straight* configuration of worship conceivable. Verse 43 is thus the offer-side of the subtraction that verse 42 had opened: what remains when the facades are cleared is God's Way, and to the son gazing at his father, the way has become a person-to-person invitation, issued in a living room.

**The Son Who Would Be Led by No Elder**

The chapter's Makkan audience — Quraysh in patrilineal formation, expert in ranking lineages — is being given an account of the genealogy of prophecy re-ordered: the true heir in a house of idols, certified truthful and prophetic (19:41), challenges for spiritual headship over his elder on the sole evidence of what he *received*. The verse legitimates, in Abraham's name, every young believer of Makkah confronting an elder's older error.

Even before the father's answer comes, his is the chapter-school's finished instruction in the ethics of asserting revelation: grounded in received knowledge, offered in service, argued without pride, declared in gentleness, and ending in direction — *a sound path*, a promised destination. The father can refuse the son's content without being able to fault the son's manner, which is exactly the leverage the next verses press to the limit.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:44

> O dear father! Do not worship Satan. Surely Satan is ever rebellious against the Most Compassionate

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict That Skips the Stones**

The third address cuts beneath the apparatus — idols, temples, inherited custom — to the agency behind them: *Do not worship Satan*. The commentators are careful and convincing on the semantic shape of the command: Abraham's people did not bow to a figure named Satan (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Thaʿlabī); what they performed was obedience — following the promptings toward wrong — and obedience in defiance of God, in theological fact, *is* worship of the prompting master. The idols on the household shelf were, in this analysis, the front office of an enterprise whose director remained unseen.

The hermeneutic depth of the phrase lies in that equation: every service of a false god resolves into service of the instigator who made it false. The Qur'an conceptualizes worship ultimately as allegiance in action, and not prostration as furniture: when the Arabs were later taunted for worshipping stones, God's answer ran the same way — their real object was always the system of obedience those stones organized (see 9:31 on taking scholars as lords through obedience). Abraham names the system out loud: the cult is a chain of command, and at the top of it is the rebel.

**Rebellious Against Mercy: The Name Heard Twice**

His charge against that director is specific: Satan is *ʿaṣiyy* — habitually insubordinate — *against the Most Compassionate*. The commentators connect this trait with the biography all his hearers knew: Iblīs commanded to prostrate and refusing (2:34; 18:50), the prototype of every later rebellion. The verse's note is therefore plain: against-the-Most-Compassionate rebellion is not a local habit of your idols but the founder's charter of their office — he began with mutiny, and recruits mutineers.

The choice of God's Name in the charge is among the sūrah's small astonishments. Discounting the basmalah, al-Raḥmān occurs in the Qur'an roughly a third as often again as in any other chapter — and here it falls from Abraham's tongue in a denunciation of Satan. The congruence is didactic: rebellion is measured by the mercy it refuses. Disobedience to the Avenger might be half-grasped as defiance of power; disobedience to the *Compassionate* is rebellion against benevolence itself, against the source of all unearned care, which is what makes Satan the exact tragic case of the sūrah's master-theme: mercy encountered and refused.

**Obedience as Worship: The Deep Rule the Chapter Teaches**

The traditional commentary on this verse (al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī) preserves a rule with endless reach: whoever obeys another in disobedience to God, *that* obedience is the person's worship. The rule disenchants much more than statues: institutions, customs, appetites — anything with the power to command action against God's limit receives worship in the act of compliance. 'I did not bow to it in a shrine,' the rule says, is no defense, because kneeling is not the only liturgy.

Abraham's own context makes the rule vivid: Azar's household-gods needed no devil-priests, no satanic invocation — everything was wrapped in decency and ancestral custom. His son's sentence unwraps it without his having to accuse his father of malice: you have been made to perform, under the etiquette of respectability, the dossier of Satan's lifelong campaign. Naming the real enemy simultaneously exonerates the description of the human target — the father is addressed, throughout, as a beloved captive of deception (compare 19:45's finish: a terrifying fate, *for him*, averted by the rescue being offered).

**The Argument After the Gifts of Intellect**

Read through the five-address structure of verses 42–45, the verse marks the pivot from rescue by reason (42: hear, see, benefit) and rescue by offer (43: follow me, I will guide) to rescue by diagnosis (44: your system has an inventor and his registration is rebellion) — with the next verse's warning of the end-state as culmination. At no point does the son redescribe the idols, because he need not: the statues had already been emptied; what his father needed now was a theory of his own system, and verse 44 provides it in one sentence.

This expositional generosity needs underlining: Abraham does not cry *heretic*; he credits the situation with enough sophistication to deserve explanation. The diagnosed father could, in principle, profit — the hidden director once named is reframed from household spirit to outlaw — and the son thereby opens the door, before the warning lands, to disavowal of the system without losing face. The verse refuses no honor to its recipient except the honor of his idols; to him it offers the revival of his own reason, pitted against the one who misled it from the beginning.

**Mercy Named Nowhere Else So Loudly**

The sūrah has taught its listener, by verse 44, to expect this Name from God at moments of care: as mercy to Zachariah's household, as Mary's refuge, as the promised Garden. Abraham delivers it differently — the Most Compassionate as the *wronged* party, mercy confronted and defied — and the chapter's theology gains another side: compassion whose rejection issues in consequence (19:45). The chapters receive their finish by the time the father answers in 46: v. 42 made the idols deaf; v. 43 reoriented the direction; v. 44 has now named the true rebel, and the one who needs distancing.

For the listening Makkans reciting their idols' litanies beside God's House, the fingering of the agency would have rung familiar: God's messengers had told them all along that their rites merchandised, under titles of intercession, the worship of the first saboteur of worship. Abraham's third dear-father address narrates the first resistance to the first idolatry of the family of prophecy, and in doing so narrates their own condition.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:45

> O dear father! I truly fear that you will be touched by a torment from the Most Compassionate, and become Satan’s companion ˹in Hell˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fear of a Son as the Final Argument**

The last of the addresses exchanges argument for confession: *I truly fear for you.* The grammar abandons proof and turns to report — Abraham testifies to his own interior, his father now the object of dread rather than refutation. Among the verse's many instructive turns, this is its subtlest: after three addresses spent on the properties of idols and the biography of Satan, the son's closing speech is nothing about God and everything about *you*. Love, having finished its syllogisms, presents its trembling.

Al-Rāzī tracks the whole sequence of words as a crescendo housed inside consistent courtesy: with each *yā abati* the trapping of danger tightens (42: worshipping what cannot act; 43: refusing available guidance; 44: obeying the first rebel; 45: nearing the appointed torment), while affection, far from tiring at the resistance, intensifies in proportion to the approaching urgency. *I am afraid for you* is a sentence at the clean opposite pole of disownment; the fear it names can exist only as long as the bond exists.

**A Torment From the Most Compassionate: The Jarring Attribution**

The punishment is attached — astonishingly, deliberately — to the same Name the sūrah has taught us to hear as mercy's: *a torment from the Most Compassionate*. The grammatical ruffle is the argument: compassion in the Qur'an is not an indulgence suspended over justice; rather, mercy's own governance encompasses consequence, which is why the sūrah can pivot within a single sentence from the Name's soothing effect to its capacity for retribution. Al-Raḥmān is not the soft accomplice of the listening tribesman; He is the guardian of the universe's moral frame, inside which persistent, knowing rejection of His outreach has real cost.

A hardship framed this way removes any picture of rival departments in God: there is no wrathful deity to appease along with a merciful one to entreat — the invitation of verse 43 and the torment of verse 45 issue from one and the same Compassionate. The son's fear for his father, then, is not of a savage fate imposed by malice but of the father's own collision with reality: to repel mercy in the direction specifically of al-Raḥmān is eventually to discover that one was arguing with the only deliverance available. The Name makes the warning more, not less, grievous.

**Friend of Satan: The Alliance He Never Knew He Had**

The destination named for the unrepentant path is performed kinship withtemp: *takūna li-l-shayṭāni waliyyan* — to become Satan's *walī*, a term denoting close alliances of allegiance, patronage, company, even friendship. The foil the chapter offers in the other direction is being God's *walī*: “There will certainly be no fear for the close servants of Allah, nor will they grieve” (10:62). Abraham's sentence divides fate along a line of intimate association: one is attached to somebody in the end — created for dependents as humans are, our final company is a matter of choice.

The commentators expose the fraud inside the verse's terror: to be Satan's *walī* is not to receive friendship in return (al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī). His nature is enmity; his clientele is abandoned precisely at the Accounting, where he lectures them on their own choosing: “I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me” (14:22). Hell is pictured in this warning as company abandoned there together — the father landing among followers of an ally who, arrival day, disavows them all. The companionship named in verse 45 is thus a companionship of desolation: attached forever to one who never loved you.

**'I Fear' as Prophetic Courtesy**

The commentators detect, inside the modest verb *akhāfu* — I fear — a species of rhetorical gentleness typical of prophecy: al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Thaʿlabī report the reading that Abraham *knows*, with prophet's certainty, the fate awaiting determined idolatry — so that *I fear* softens into idiom the knowledge of certainty, transposing the verdict into the register of anxious care. The form lets information attend the father with its seriousness undiminished, even while its person-to-person setting stays son-like.

Whether the verb is courtesy of expression or genuineness of suspension — God may turn the father where He will, and many a stubborn man has turned — the psychological substance is the same: the prophet registers the peril of his kin as personal pain. Later generations of believers in hostile families will recognize the sentence's pain and permission both at once: fearing a beloved elder's end without disowning the elder is lawful, and the prediction of ruin for the persistently rebellious does not by itself impeach the filial bond. On the contrary, the bond is displayed as the very thing that will not let the son go silent.

**The Finished Portrait Before the Answer**

By the end of the verse the sūrah's forensic portrait of ideal dawah-kinship is finished: addresses 42–43 gave reasonings and offer, 44 gave diagnosis, 45 has given warning voiced through love; and with the *dear father* still undefected from the son's lips, the dialogue passes to its second actor. From outside the text, the chapter's auditor now knows the real question verse 46 will answer: what does the threatened do — when love has provided the full address-book of rescue and the household gods are empty, when the name of the true rebel is known, and when mercy and torment share one Name?

Nor is the lesson merely ancient. For the Prophet's companions hearing these recitations amid their own fathers' hostile councils — for every believing child in every estranged house since — verse 45 is simultaneously description and warrant: a son can fear for a father, gently, without surrender, and the sentence that closes the argument can still have affection as its syntax, even at the price of being stoned out of the door (19:46).

## Sūrah Maryam 19:46

> He threatened, “How dare you reject my idols, O Abraham! If you do not desist, I will certainly stone you ˹to death˺. So be gone from me for a long time!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Words a Father Finds When He Has No Answer**

The father's reply arrives, and the sūrah sets the two speeches side by side without comment, letting the registers convict themselves. Five times addressed as *dear father*, the elder answers with a string of hardnesses: *ar-rāghibun* — do you turn from, desert — *my gods*, the threat of stoning, and a sentence of long banishment. Nothing in the reply engages a single point the son has made: the idols' deafness, the available guidance, the name of the real enemy, the fear of the son for him. All five of the son's arguments disappear unanswered into his vehemence.

The Book's presentation of the immortal scene needs no editorializing because the presented asymmetry does the work. The defense was courteous, sourced, tiered; the response is the refusal of consideration itself, wrapped in the oldest tribal logic supplied to an elder: age's writ against the younger's speech. There is a strong pattern in the chapter: where God's party reasons gently and God's enemies answer with force — the crowd against Mary (19:27–28), the father against his son — the narrative dispenses its verdict by the order of phrases alone. The father's collapse into threat is the chapter's proof that the son's case was completed.

**'My Gods': The Prophetical Possessive**

Azar's possessive pronoun repays attention: he says *my idols* — of Abraham he asks, astounded, if he *turns away from my gods*. The word choice betrays more than polytheism; it betrays investment. The household's gods are owned — by tribe, by custom, by memory — and the speech of their ownership is the father's: *mine*, as in credentialed fact by inheritance. Commentators note how often in the Book an opponent's possessives tell the truth unbelieved by them: 'our gods,' 'our fathers' religion,' 'our way' — the pronouns testify that these attachments are possessions, not truths. The dealer cannot recommend his product without naming the proprietorship.

The irony is that a claim against the idols in the son's mouth has never been about property; the son asks after faculties, after hearing, seeing, benefit. The father reframes the challenge as a challenge to *his* things — the ego tangled in the rite — and thereby clarifies why argument could not reach him: he was defending himself. Apostasy from idols, in Azar's ear, registers as dispossession of Azar, and from there the argument has nowhere to go but threat. The possession of religion is the possession that prevents religion's rescue — a pathology the Qur'an diagnoses from beneath many different masks.

**Stoning: The Static Reply of Prophethood's Enemies**

The threat named — *I shall surely stone you* — has its debaters in detail (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī). The majority read of the commentators carries it as 'I will curse and revile you,' — a stoning by words — the verb's force used regularly in Arabic for a volley of punishment by tongue; yet the possibility of literal stoning is also transmitted (al-Qurṭubī) and was a live manner of execution in the region's past, as Abraham's peoples also practiced the fire-built pyre (21:68; 37:97 — for attempted burning with fire).

Whether words or stones — or both — the threat is continuous with the prophetic history of stymied power: Noah's people answer him in identical terms: “They threatened, ‘If you do not desist, O Noah, you will surely be stoned ˹to death˺’” (26:116). God makes the threat-formula itself cross millennia into uniformity. When the arguments of heaven find no rebuttal, the regimes of earth reach for the last argument, and its name, whatever the century, is violence-syllabized-as-answer. Once again, the chapter preaches by juxtaposition: a son who has nothing but disclosed sources, and a father who reaches straight for the stones. The audience knows whose side the chapter is on before either party finishes.

**Banished for an Extended Season**

The sentence has one more clause, and its idioms are heavy with pathos: *take leave of me for a long while* — *ihjurnī maliyyan*. Maliyyan covers extended time — long banishment, or deep repudiation — a dismissalness measured in years he knows he cannot live; al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Thaʿlabī also keep the more merciful gloss: the father banishes his son *so that the father cannot punish him* — exile as substitute for harm, as if behind the tyranny a remnant of paternal protection held back the worst by ordering it away.

Whichever sense prevails, the exile clause is the verse's hinge into the sūrah's master-motif of driven-out mercy: Zachariah away, Mary east and out, Jesus' shelter under the palm, and now Abraham put at distance for refusing gods. The Qur'an's consistent doctrine expounded for the Makkan believers — themselves preparing the Hijrah as the recitation proceeds — is that departure from hostile home can be the appointed road to blessing rather than its interruption. Azar intends his *leave me* as dismissal; the chapter receives it as the father's consent to a departure God will turn into gifts (19:49).

**The Threatening as the Chapter's Point of Inversion**

From verses 42 through 45 the dialogue held its son-attentive register — kindness, analysis, offer, fear. Verse 46 represents the occasion on which the traditional order itself is indicted: in the name of the household gods, the honorable father answers the prophet son with the threat of death by stones. And the sūrah, without losing its tone's discipline, notes the answer in one uninterrupted, damnatory breath.

For a Makkan audience, poised between ancestral proud furniture and the recited God, the verse draws the only line that matters: refusal to rebut can turn tribal authority violent, no matter how padded by household robes. What the son will say next — the Book's most civilized acceptance of expulsion (19:47) — is legible only against this: that in the face of *I will stone you*, courtesy holds its ground, and leaves.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:47

> Abraham responded, “Peace be upon you! I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness. He has truly been Most Gracious to me

**Expanded Commentary**

**Peace Said at the Point of Departure**

The son's reply to threatened stones is a salutation: *Peace be upon you* — *salāmun ʿalayka*. The commentators are exact about its function: not a friendly hello, but a close-of-encounter word — a declaration that he will not fight, that no harm will come from his side, and that confrontation, not kinship, is what ends here (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī). Remarkable etiquette is at work: the offensive against his faith was answered with reason; the threat against his person is answered with peace. Wit will not be escalated into woundedness, nor rebuke grudged into rancor.

The phrase belongs to a whole revealed etiquette of parting from hostile ignorance, later made a standing rule for the believers: “The ˹true˺ servants of the Most Compassionate are those who walk on the earth humbly, and when the foolish address them ˹improperly˺, they only respond with peace” (25:63); “When they hear slanderous talk, they turn away from it, saying, ‘We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours. Peace ˹is our only response˺ to you!’” (28:55). Abraham at the threshold of his father's house is the exemplar of both codified verses; the prophetic community in Makkah — practicing the same restraint in the same years — would have heard the law and the biography coinciding.

**Praying for the Persecutor**

The next clause converts farewell into intercession: *I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness*. A son who just heard stones threatened offers, as his final household service, advocacy for the one who threatened. Commentators note the completeness of the offer's righteousness: Abraham, formally wronged in his duty's discharge, chooses his strongest remaining instrument on his father's behalf — supplication — which costs the persecutor nothing and the persecuted everything except pride.

The Book, however, also maps this intercession's moral boundary with precision and explains its later course: “As for Abraham's prayer for his father's forgiveness, it was only in fulfilment of a promise he had made to him. But when it became clear to Abraham that his father was an enemy of Allah, he broke ties with him. Abraham was truly tender-hearted, forbearing” (9:114). Intercession for the hostile is a duty as long as hope is alive; it lapses when God's decree discloses the hardening as final. The fulness here of Abraham's innocence — before the disclosure — is exactly the image 9:114 appeals to in his defense; *tender-hearted, forbearing* are God's own epitaph on this scene.

**He Has Been Most Gracious to Me**

A third clause moves from intercession to testimony: *He has truly been Most Gracious to me* — God, that is — the pronoun quiet in its certainty. The son who is being stripped of house and name here affirms that the One he worships has habituated him to kindness, and he has his own history as the exhibit. The disconnect is pointed: the father's possession of sons was the usual measure of God's favor in their world; and this son is being expelled, yet affirms that he has been enfolded in grace. Grace in God's register is evidently not household-tested.

The commentators render *ḥafiyyan* — gracious, kindness-constant — back into their preferred register of prophetology: God's intimate gentle care accompanying the prophets in their passages (cf. the comfort under the palm (19:24) and the whole career of these mercies in the sūrah). Spoken inside a room of threatened violence, the clause is also polemic again: the stones of Azar have no power over *this* son, because between him and harm stands one whose name runs Mercy; and the father is thereby being answeringly invited, one more time, to identify with that same protection, since a father's household blessing, too, stands under the kind One who has treated the son well.

**A Composite Poise the Chapter Measures Word by Word**

Hardness in this scene is entirely one-sided, yet no clause of the son's reply drifts into self-protection. Each of the three performs a separate moral task, and the sum totals a complete posture under persecution: *peace* disengages the quarrel without endorsing the wrong; the intercession keeps the heart free of vengeance, since a man cannot both curse a household and pray for it; and the testimony of grace fixes his own identity safe in God's care rather than in the house that is expelling him. The three together are a believer's exit-visa from any prison of bitterness.

Later revelation would distribute these three acts separately as communal law for the Prophet's followers at their own thresholds of expulsion — peace to the ignorant (25:63), prayer for the struggling, testimony of God's goodness in hardship (2:152 — cited in theme). Verse 47 compresses them into one sentence and so reveals their common root: all are fragments of a single interior freedom, the freedom of the one whose file is held elsewhere. What the father thought to deprive — name, home, security — the son's three clauses declare not to be his to give or take.

**The Greeting That Measured a Household**

As al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note, this is the salutation the believers are taught in the world to come as well as given in this one — the residents of Paradise greet peace (7:46; 10:10) — and its usage in verse 47 heavily seals the son's verdict on the encounter: with *peace* he identifies the salvation-line down which family and hostilities have come. When those heavenly greetings are spoken on the Last Day, on which side of it this father stands has yet to be decided — this is precisely why intercession continues: peace now holds open a door so peace may be said across it later.

All three clauses of the verse finally organize into the chapter's most complete lesson on how to lose at home without losing oneself: refuse enmity (*peace be upon you*); go on interceding for the still-living wrongdoer against himself (*I will pray... forgiveness*); and hold one's worship in the present tense of personal witness (*He has been gracious to me*). No bitterness, no beheading, no surrender either: Abraham leaves the house speaking in one sentence the whole content of the distribution ahead — peace for both, prayer for one, gratitude held openly over a life only just stripped of its earthly certainties.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:48

> As I distance myself from ˹all of˺ you and from whatever you invoke besides Allah, I will ˹continue to˺ call upon my Lord ˹alone˺, trusting that I will never be disappointed in invoking my Lord.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Withdrawal Finished Without Hatred**

The son's exit is executed in grammar as precisely as it was defended in argument: *I distance myself from you* — *wa-aʿtazilukum* — and simultaneously from what they called on besides God. Withdrawal is thereby doubled: from the people and from their objects of devotion. In classical Qur'anic science this is the doctrine later gathered under *barāʾah* — disassociation — a cutting whose dignity here consists in its being unshouted. No denunciating closing lecture, no rehearsal of wounds: quietly announced separation, aimed at the source of the sick relationship and not the father's person.

The Qur'an enshrines the same syllabic recipe in its general verdict over the whole Abrahamic community of resistance: “‘We totally dissociate ourselves from you and ˹shun˺ whatever ˹idols˺ you worship besides Allah. We reject you’” (60:4). Verse 48 is the autobiographical original of that communal formula — apostolic dissociation held in the normative first person singular. Gentler than a curse, firmer than reconciliation at any price, it gives the alienated believer's life a legislated shape: distance spelled out, without venom; faith pronounced aloud at the point of exit.

**What He Will Not Leave: The Invocation of His Lord**

With one renunciation complete, the verse's counterweight follows, and the response is a posture, not a protest: *I will call upon my Lord, trusting that I will never be disappointed in invoking my Lord.* In that word, withdrawal is not bare subtraction but a re-investment: the social companionship stripped away is paid for in advance with the promise — spoken in hope's own grammar — of a solace in the direct line of God (al-Qurṭubī's preferred interpretation: prayer for family and posterity to fill the new solitude).

Those notes about the content of his supplication have a doubleness about them. Some commentators propose that his plea was for the father's guidance; but as al-Qurṭubī notes, the bulkier probability lies with the surrounding narrative of the sūrah — Abraham, freshly expelled, petitions for family and supporters of his own, for a lineage to replace the one that has stoned him out. The sequel (19:49) will count as God's audit of that prayer. And so the verse's chain of syntax becomes the chapter's sequence of consolation: dissociate from what is hostile to God, ask God for company, and find that one petition cited into a family of prophets.

**Never Wretched at the Door of Prayer**

The final clause, *never disappointed … in invoking my Lord*, is the key to the verse's inner architecture and a deliberate echo of the prayer of verse 4. Zachariah, at the head of the sūrah, had spoken the identical testimony: “but I have never been disappointed in my prayer to You” (19:4). The pairing of the two men across the chapter is made through the same declension of hope: the prophet of Mary's guardian before, and the prophet of Islam's first family now — each certifying for Himself that God's door does not distribute disappointment.

In the Christian-colored circles of the sūrah's later recitation, the point too would register its weight. Tribal rejection of all sorts — anyone driven out for confusing the sincerest forms of faith — is issued here the specific antidote God's prophets keep from one another: the assessment of prayer based on consecutive answers received. Not blind optimism, but the ledger of an established history: *I have asked; He has, always, heard; therefore I trust of the future what I know of the past.* The dispersed man contains in himself, owing to his prayer-file, a sure collateral against isolation.

**The Father's Language Abandoned, the Father's God Addressed**

The attentive listener could also hear — through the pronouns of the address — what this kind of solitude was going to look like: you invoke *whatever apart from God*, he states, so I will call on *my Lord*. The possessive *rabbī* — my Lord — in the exile's sentence echoes Zachariah's exact address in verse 4 rather than Abraham's tribal-household deities, and it signals possession of a relation that cannot be taken away by exile (compare 19:4). Such is the religion of the driven: possession where it can be held — in the vocative; and loss of it nowhere.

The Abrahamic rhythm of the Qur'an is here, as everywhere, also the convert's rhythm: count off the devotions you can no longer socially share; decouple yourself gently from them; enter on a private schedule of worship "alone" with your Lord; and entrust company, family, and children to the One who answers invocations as He answered ʿabdhu Zachariah's. The Abraham of Sūrah Maryam is precisely the Prophet of leaving-behind, of a God who is left *with*.

**A Son, Exiled, Alone, and Consoled in Advance**

The careful listener of the sūrah will register also that the sequence parallels earlier scenes: Mary had retreated from people (19:16) and had her answer *with her*; Mary's son will soon be found homeless in his itinerancy (19:32's commentarial tradition); the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ is simultaneously in his Makkan harassments. Abraham, in 48, enters into this family of God's assisted solitaries, and the verse's poise — leaving them to their devices, himself to prayers — is the whole lesson in the ethics of forced migration ahead of the Hijrah its readers were preparing.

The vacuum his exit opens is precisely the vacancy of the rest of the chapter: what fills the fatherless household, God will declare in verse 49 — not a recantation, not companions of the old idols, but new bonds issued from the place the exiled just claimed his safety: invocations answered, a lineage raised from his prayer.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:49

> So after he had left them and what they worshipped besides Allah, We granted him Isaac and Jacob, and made each of them a prophet

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Turns Exile Into Endowment**

The coordination is plain in the Arabic: *when* he pulled away from them, *We bestowed* on him. The temporal connective is also causal in effect: the departure and the gift are knit in one act of divine counter-supply (al-Ṭabarī), and the shape recurs through this sūrah's evaluations of consecrated leaving: of Zachariah's lonely prayer (a son, 19:7), of Mary's isolated withdrawal (a son, 19:19), of Abraham's expulsion (sons, 19:49). The chapter's doctrine is being instantiated as pattern: whoever consents for God's sake to lose his people is met at the far side by a family of God's appointing.

The commentators describe the balancing in immediate pastoral terms: the gift of Isaac and Jacob was compensation, a replacement of what had been surrendered (al-Ṭabarī), and moreover an exchange *better than what was lost* — a judgment to be repeated, amplified at the level of legacy, in the following verse's account of worldwide renown. Whatever the loss-ledger of the father Azar, its entries by God's re-evaluation close in surpluses of prophetic lineage. A deceived house, a hostile tribe, the machinery of custom — a man can be cut loose of them all, but he may not thereby be *cut into shortage*: "after" in God's bookkeeping turns out to hold "greater."

**Isaac: The Son of the Barren Years and the Laughed Promise**

The first name needs little introduction to the Book's audience, but the chapter's allusiveness asks the listener to supply the whole file: Sarah's laughter in the tent (11:71; 51:29–30), Abraham in old age — “All praise is for Allah who has blessed me with Ishmael and Isaac in my old age” (14:39) — all previously graded as impossible in Abraham's own annunciation structure. On the sūrah's logic developed through Zachariah, Isaac is another case-study of ease performing against the arithmetic of nature (19:9): heirs from barrenness, twice in twenty verses.

The gift-halting school of al-Ṭabarī (cf. 6:84 — “And We blessed him with Isaac and Jacob. We guided them all as We previously guided Noah...”) underlines a layer apt in the sūrah's progression: Isaac is the *covenant-son*, continuator of the Abrahamic household in the settled line, immediately becoming the one through whom a whole race of prophets would descend (29:27: prophecy and Scripture reserved for his descendants). The verse's mention of him first is also tactical: in one name, God's repossession of Abraham from his Babylonian loss is inclusive of a far-extending future — not just the gift of a child but a dynasty of prophets (21:72).

**Jacob: A Grandson Appointed Before His Time**

The subtler surprise of the verse is the second name: Jacob — a grandson — is listed in the very bestowal clause: *We bestowed on him Isaac and Jacob.* Such grammar deals in God's foreknowledge before the human experience: the gift includes, in anticipation, descendants not yet born to the father newly granted a son. Hearing by grace, Abraham receives not only a child but an announced continuity — two generations of prophecy issued in answer to one exile.

The tafsīr tradition registers the exactness here too: “And We blessed him with Isaac ˹as a son˺ and Jacob ˹as a grandson˺, as an additional favour” (21:72). The anticipatory Jacob is God's signature that leaving-for-God settles debts at generational depth — the exile from polytheism is granted to see not merely a son but the line beyond the son; assurance of succession which, like Zachariah's, calms the permanent loneliness of a believer's detachments at a stroke. Ceding his father, Abraham receives in one clause son and grandson.

**'And Each We Made a Prophet': Converting Gifts to Offices**

The verse's last clause counts off the value of the gift on its own scale: *and each We made a prophet*. The pronoun *kull* — each — extends, the commentators note (al-Ṭabarī), sensibly over the named and the named gifts — Abraham himself included — which amounts to a plural confirmation of rank: the whole Abrahamic stem is hereby constituted as a prophetic house. The same statement repeated of the broader line at 6:84–89 and at 29:27 installs it as the permanent Qur'anic characteristic of his line: the reservation of prophecy and scripture to Abraham's seed.

Materially, the clause establishes that gifts from God in the sūrah are not breaks from prophetic duty but an intensification of it. Retirement into the consolations of a family secured is not Abraham's covenant; what God sends into the space left by breaking with the father is *children charged with His office*. Between the son(s) of the believer's prayer and the fate of those children stands God's qualification: they are given both as gift and as charge, and their continuing the legacy of leaving-for-God is part of their function. Sūrah-style mercy shown *to* prophets is simultaneously mercy installed *through* them for others.

**The Chapter's Arithmetic of Leaving**

Stepping back, the verse completes the chapter's great proof concerning separation: everything Abraham renounced with such precise gentleness (19:47–48) has been its perfect counterweight. The formula arrived before at verse 48 — 'I will call on my Lord; trusting I will not be wretched' — and the divine sentence that follows in verse 49 reads as the liable clause of the contract the believer's prayer had signed: *when he left them*, God's naming of compensation starts at once.

History, the sūrah implies at all levels, is in Abraham's image: Mary withdrew, and from withdrawing received provision; the Muslims of Makkah would shortly leave, and from leaving gain a city. God's arithmetic therefore stands printed for every reader called to choose between inherited attachments and God's Way: loss registered, then replaced, then exceeded — the shrewdly kept account of the Bountiful One, who, in this chapter, keeps displaying His habit of generosity to exiles.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:50

> We showered them with Our mercy, and blessed them with honourable mention

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mercy Invested Over a Whole Household**

The first clause of the crown is a distribution: *We granted them of Our mercy.* The plural reaches past Abraham's person across the family just bestowed — Isaac and Jacob, the father and the house — and the commentary divides honestly on the composition of the grant: world-side mercy, namely bounty and provision, children and prosperity (al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī), and then a carrying forward of the same account into prophet-side mercy — revelation and office folded in among the gifts (al-Thaʿlabī, cf. 29:27). Both phases belong to this sūrah's analyzed picture; mercy in this chapter is explicable only as double entry: kindness in what creatures need (children, lands), kindness in the cause to which creatures are called (prophethood, scripture).

This first plural matters as well to the chapter's architecture of proof. For the individual instances of mercy in prior verses (Zachariah's son, Mary's provision), God here distributes mercy *housewide*: the mercy with which the sūrah's account opened over a single servant (19:2) closes this segment over a household, marking the scale of God's answer to the father-exile. Once one man consented to isolation with Heaven for the sake of God, the mercy that found him kept growing in circles outward — first his child, then the line, then their cause.

**Lisān Ṣidq: A Tongue of Truth as the Second Mercy**

The second clause is Arabic idiom of dense filigree, translatable only by unpacking its metaphor: *lisāna ṣidqin ʿaliyyan* — a tongue of truthfulness, elevated, sublime. Literally the phrase pictures a tongue — the voice by which men speak of you — of truth and high standing, and its meaning in the mouths of the commentators is exactly that: *honourable mention*, reputation, the sincere praise of the truthful (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Ṭabrisī). God installed Abraham and his progeny as an article of the world's speech — an entry in the ongoing conversation of humanity, and installed it high: the family would simply never be without celebrant tongues.

Honor in this clause is precisely calibrated; it is *of ṣidq*, truth. The publicity is not fame in the sense the moderns exhaust, not notoriety of a great house or remarkable bloodline; it is *true speech abroad*, by generations, about who Abraham was and what God gave his line. The opposite can also exist — a tongue of *falsehood* raised over the genuinely righteous is history's other routine; Mary's own case supplies the sūrah's adjacent foil (19:27–28), as the town's tongue was false before God issued *His* tongue in the sūrah. Abraham's house receives the one cure for both prospects: truth spoken about them *sublimely*, by God's arrangement, *forever after*.

**The Prayer, Requested in Advance, Is Answered Here**

The commentators connect the gift to its identical request — Abraham had already asked, in terms practically verbatim: “Bless me with honourable mention among later generations” (26:84). Between the prayer in Sūrah 26 and this clause of Sūrah 19 stands the whole covenantal method of the Abraham file: requests are registered across the Qur'an like instruments of a long-case account, and payments arrive where God's compilation has them properly falling due; the prayer for honorable mention ripens in the statement of its fulfillment.

The intertextuality also discloses the prayer's assessment by God. Abraham had asked for mention *among later generations* — that is, for something slight in the world of present-tense power and fifty-six generations later still debated by empires and recitation daily. The response stretches the petition past patriarchal imagination: his name fills the daily formula of Muslim prayer (in which God's blessing is invoked on him: 'Allāhumma ṣalli ʿalā Muḥammadin wa ʿalā āli Muḥammadin kamā ṣallayta ʿalā Ibrāhīma…' in the concluding tashahhud); recited in the abbey as often as in the ribat; held by Jew, Christian, Muslim, and acknowledged even by the pagan Arabs of the Prophet's time, who recognized Abraham as venerable forefather. News of the verse's fulfillment has simply never gone out of print.

**Universality as the Trademark of Abrahamic Honor**

The commentators explicitly extend the circle of the elevated tongue across confessional lines: not Jews alone, Christians alone, or Muslims alone, but the full procession of God's sections among mankind, with even the pre-Islamic idolatrous Arabs agreeing to Abraham's nobility (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Ṭabrisī). No other figure in this sūrah's history attains this exact zoological completeness of positive mention across rival platforms. The renown itself presages Islam's public logic: God wires into world-history one man whose memory monotheists cannot reject and receives *that* man's rebuttal of polytheism — “Why do you worship…?” (19:42) — as the permanent dialectic of Near-Eastern religion.

And so the second mercy of the verse — *honorable mention* — becomes itself an instrument of preaching: the most-told father, whose file includes the tender and final refutation of idolatry, has already been given by God to every party as its own. Dispute him, and a party abandons its own honored register of fathers; own him, and his central case stands unanswerably in its archive. The “elevated tongue of truthfulness” is thus no fated flattery but is itemized among the sūrah's distributions of mercy, a mercy extended to all the world, since from it each generation receives the same exhortation to be delivered from idolatry through Abraham's own honored witness.

**Withdrawal, Vindicated Into the Tongue of Ages**

Al-Shādhilī and Ibn ʿAjībah find, and the sūrah's cadence supports, the chapter's full arc inside these two mercies of verse 50: withdrawal from men answered at last by having God's mercy descend and then the genuine speech about you elevated to — and lodged on — the tongues of ages. Solitude in its season; then an honor, a truth — God's own regime about those who leave for His sake what cost them their all.

The sufi commentary gives the arc its spiritual direction: abandoning creatures for the company of the Truth is, they teach, the appointed beginning of the path, and *with such beginning* attaches — in God's knowing instance — the lifting of veils, the descent of mercy, love realized, and a *tongue of truthfulness* in due time. For Abraham the time was given in this world: a world's speech of him in generations, and the sons of his prosperity as prophets. Between the threat of a father's stones (19:46) and universal mention (19:50), the sūrah has carried its greatest son out of his hostile household and set him up as humanity's own.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:51

> And mention in the Book ˹O Prophet, the story of˺ Moses. He was truly a chosen man, and was a messenger and a prophet

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mention: The Scroll Turns From the Father to the Deliverer**

The imperative *mention* opens the third file of the patriarchal gallery, and its addressee carries continuing weight: the Prophet ﷺ is to recall Moses — in a sūrah read out by a minority beset by overwhelming force, the biography of prophecy's most-documented refugee. The chapter's movement from Abraham to Moses is built into the genealogy of the audience itself: the Makkans honored both — Abraham by lineage, Moses by the prestige of his scripture among the People of the Book — and the recitation gathers them, one after the other, into the same treasury of evidence for mercy toward God's exiles.

Moses needs no narrative summary in this chapter and receives none: the Qur'an's substantive biographies of him fill other sūrahs (7, 20, 26, 28), and here his file is a concentrated elevation to the chapter's theme: chosenness, speech with God, brotherly gift. God selects familiar national material and stamps it into the sūrah's register of gentle favors — Moses' nearness at the Mount for every prophet's audience, Aaron's appending as mercy for all laboring in God's call. Mentioning Moses here — without narrative across twenty sūrah-lines — makes his story the chapter's shortest evidence that this *sūrah of mercy* extends also to the warrior-prophets.

**Mukhliṣ, Mukhlaṣ: A Man Devoted — or a Man Devotedly Chosen**

The verse describes Moses with a word whose two readings the commentators keep deliberately in suspension: *mukhliṣan*, one utterly devoted — worshipping God alone, without hypocrisy (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī) or *mukhlaṣan*, made choice, picked out, specially raised to God's favor. Al-Ṭabarī's weight is with the position that both hold: the faithful tradition retains the pair as a single description under two vocalizations, visible together — the man devoted to his God and the man his God specially designated.

Devotion, in turn, has its spiritual definition recorded by the Sufis (Ibn ʿAjībah): sincerity is to worship God for God's sake, not for blessings in this world or the next — a reading that transposes the narrative elevated term into the ethical: devotion as purity of motive, purified of tariff. Whether 'sincere' or 'selected' or both, Moses' file in the sūrah is reduced to a portrait of single-heartedness so pure that later aspiration adopted the word as the summit of its vocabulary of approaching God. The sūrah has, in one epithet, previewed the whole life of hidden devotion: a prophet who sought and found his Lord away from Egypt's spotlight, among Midian's herds.

**Messenger and Prophet: The Rank That Reaches Two Nations**

Then comes the formal notation of office: *a messenger and a prophet* — both titles. The commentators' notes turn precisely exact here (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī): messenger (rasūl) by the fact of the Torah — a bearer of a new scripture and a new law; and prophet (nabī) in the general prophetic function reaching two peoples: his own Israelites and the Egyptian establishment of Pharaoh. One man stands within both diplomas of the divine diplomatic service.

The ranking is also a service the chapter renders the whole argument of mercy: designation of this weight — *choicely chosen and both offices*, after so modest a life in Midian — is God's first endorsement of Moses' lowly years as the true measuring-stick of his greatness. What looks, in the Egyptian archive, like an escaped murderer's late return appears in the sūrah's register as the education of a messenger-prophet. God's mercy's habit of brief notation — mentioned in each of these mini-files — already reads in the verse: the biography's hidden years count; the titles that follow them are appointed by One who was schooling him there.

**The Most-Narrated Prophet in the Book**

Moses is the prophet whose account the Qur'an retells more than any other — over a dozen major passages and hundreds of verses — and the chapter's impulse toward economy is thus deliberate: while he bulks elsewhere with long chronicle scenes (7:103–155; 10:75–93; 20:9–97; 26:10–67; 28:3–46), his file in the chapter of mercy is a few strokes — epithet, office, mountain, brother. In the economy of the sūrah, Moses is present as one *proven* case among the chapter's total registry: mercy accompanies the refugee, gives him rank, gives him family-support, gives him speech in God's presence.

The recitation in Makkah gathers a double charge from this economy: the audience will not miss that, amid the chapter's all-but-obvious parallels, Moses' story is the story of standing-alone-against-extraordinary-power — exactly the condition of the small Muslim community then. And besides this topicality, Moses' file in miniature instructs in the mode of the quranic *dhikr*: memory directed at landmarks, not exhaustiveness. God calls out not by recounting to the last detail but by recalling precisely what the moment needs.

**What the Gallery Needs Moses For**

The chapter has before this verse presented a pattern of bestowals marking its generous God: sons to the aged (19:7), sons as gifts from divine decree (19:49), sons-in-the-cradle as signs (19:30). Moses introduces a completely different category of mercy — enhancement by office: chosenness, rank, speech, brother-support — a mercy that elevates not after weakness of body alone but toward assignment in struggle; Pharaoh's court is the next verse's backdrop, and mercy dresses him for it.

In the arrangement of the gallery — Abraham the disputant at home, Moses the disputant at empire — the chapter's pedagogy broadens its own comfort: exile is not always departure toward familial consolation; sometimes it is the preparation for confrontation, issued in God's chosen company. Moses, then, is the chapter's expansion of mercy's register from gifts to assignments: God bestows not only around the fire of the hearth (sons, provision), but in the heat of the call (offices, protection, and — as the next verse preserves — voice from above).

## Sūrah Maryam 19:52

> We called him from the right side of Mount Ṭûr, and drew him near, speaking ˹with him˺ directly

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call on the Right Side of the Valley**

The verse maps one of God's celebrated appointments in the Book: *We called him from the right side of Mount Ṭûr*. The commentators locate the phrase geographically with precision: while Moses, traveling from Midian with his family, approached the sacred area, the side of the valley that lay to his right (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī) — the fuller account gives the details of fire spotted in the night and the bush in the blessed sector: “But when he came to it, he was called from the bush in the sacred ground to the right side of the valley: ‘O Moses! It is truly I. I am Allah—the Lord of all worlds’” (28:30; cf. 20:11–12; 27:8).

The spatial register matters because the Qur'an keeps God's manifestations habitually at limits. Meaning gathers at thresholds: the shore, the tree, the night, the right side of the valley. Geography is lighted with holiness by the appointment, not by natural qualities; and transliteration *Ṭûr* is the Book's own runner-name for Sinai (Ṭūr Sīnīn, 23:20; 95:2), the mountain that became the chapter-country of all later theophanies. 'Right side' in turn taps the sūrah's and scripture's register of the auspicious (yumn), and the most earned pun in tafsīr: the *ayman* (right) of the valley receives the man who is being put at God's *right side* in the chapter's own fortunes.

**Intimate Discourse at the Summit of Prophethood**

The second clause — *We drew him near, speaking with him directly* — describes the dialogic core of Moses' distinction. *Najiyya* denotes an interior, private colloquy — talk apart, talk without go-between, talk in near company — and the commentators use its possibilities lavishly. On the most often repeated image, Moses was brought so close that he could hear the scratching of the pen against the tablets of the Torah (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī); on others, he was raised to hear the speech of God Himself (al-Ṭabrisī), a scene that answers at once for the famous Qur'anic certification of his rank: “And to Moses Allah spoke directly” (4:164).

From that conversation is born Moses' standing honorific: Kalīm Allāh — the one whom God addressed. Not every prophet, in the schools' reading, received the unmediated word in waking, face-to-face appointment; Moses' kalām-occasion is described also outside this sūrah (4:164) with the same isolation of sense: among messengers, he stands singled by God's direct speech. The sūrah inserts the scene into its own thematic count of mercies — as the answer of heaven to one man's kindled career: after fire in the desert and before Pharaoh's court, there is an interval in which God simply talked with him.

**Nearness That Asks No Location**

The spiritually minded commentators are at their most awake over the clause *We drew him near*. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabrisī take care that proximity is measured in offices, not in cubits — raised rank, increased nobility, reception into special intimacy — since God is not contained by place or distance; nearness here is honor as contrasted with ordinary distance of the creature from revelation. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī preserve the tradition's more pictorial form: seventy thousand veils of light and darkness stretch between God and creation, and Moses came so near that a single veil stood between himself and his Lord.

In the hands of the mystics of the quranic tafsir tradition (the al-Kāshānī preserves the widest), the right side of Ṭûr becomes the most interior valley of the heart itself, the chamber where God's address reaches the human spirit past its filters; and the reader then holds the twofold sense together: geography blessed once in history, and the interior possibility it signals. Neither class pollutes the other; together they trace a nearness that can be honored both in legal exactitude of creed and in the devotion of the journeying soul.

**The Mountain Where Petition Turns to Ordinance**

It deserves pause that this is the second right-side appointment in revelation's memory of Moses, and the order of the two is theological: before he can be *sent*, he must be *summoned*. The sequence the fuller accounts preserve is a fugitive, a fire seen at night, a voice, a command to remove the sandals at the holy valley Ṭuwā (20:11–12), and then — and only then — the commissioning. God teaches, through the mountain's ritual geography, that authority for the mission is consecrated first: the staff and the signs come to a man already standing barefoot in servitude.

The chapter's first audience of pilgrims would know a parallel of their own: God's house at Makkah likewise asks a man to arrive, disrobe from rank, circle and run and stand before he is dispatched home. Revelation keeps God's sancta as places that take a man apart before raising him, and Ṭûr is the ancestor of them all. Verse 52 carries that sanctuary-law into the gallery of mercy: before God issued law to a nation, He had a man draw near, alone.

**The Hearth of an Empire-Confronting Prophet**

This brief mountain inventory performs a perfectly calculated office in the chapter. Moses' case has been, of the whole gallery, the one least evidently about gifts for gifts' sake: his mercy had a mission in it — bearing a law to a hardened, afflicted nation. The right-side-of-Ṭûr colloquy is the armory from which the mission is issued: commandments finished in private conversation, a prepared code, strength to take back.

So seen, verse 52 becomes the chapter's lesson in how God's gifts serve as preparation. Zachariah's son is issued to guarding the temple; Mary's son to preaching from the cradle; Abraham's sons to building a nation; Moses' colloquy to facing an empire. In each case mercy arrives *defined by the task ahead of it*; in Moses' case most explicitly, since, as verse 53 will unfold, God will even apportion part of the mission to a brother — and the summit-theophany is His first and top provision of that appointment.

## Sūrah Maryam 19:53

> And We appointed for him—out of Our grace—his brother, Aaron, as a prophet

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gift Clause of the Chapter, Completed in a Brother**

The verse re-opens the sūrah's governing grammar of mercy: *We granted him* — by Our wahb, the same bestowal-word used across the chapter for John to Zachariah, Isaac-and-Jacob to Abraham, and the child to Mary. Yet the object here is not a son but a brother, and not merely kin but *a prophet*. The Quran's gallery now stages its most interpersonal mercy: God's answer to loneliness-in-mission is the arrival of a second commissioned man. Zachariah got an heir; Abraham got a household; Moses gets a co-worker.

The placement — *out of Our grace* — is repeated deliberately across these verses (cf. 19:49–50): mercy is being itemized as a register of incoming gifts, and Aaron belongs on that ledger like any miraculous child. Brotherhood rises to the rank of raḥma: God's gift of companionship inside vocation. One man's yoke is made plural. Later tradition will find in the verse the standing proof that God rarely sends significant callers one by one — the work is too heavy, so the mercy is structurally fraternal.

**The Prayer That Asked for Exactly This**

Aaron's commissioning is no sudden masterstroke; rather, it is a direct and named answer to Moses' explicit petition — preserved elsewhere, at Sūrah Ṭā-Hā's founding appointment: Moses was told to go to Pharaoh, and he prayed, “and grant me a helper from my family, Aaron, my brother. Strengthen me through him” (20:29–31), seeking inclusion of his brother in the task (20:32). God's reply there is instant: “Your request is granted, O Moses!” (20:36). The present verse is the chapter's notation of that granted request: Aaron entered prophethood not by his own annunciation scene, but through his brother's prayer.

The reason Moses gave is preserved as well, and it is as profound as transparent: “And my brother Aaron is more eloquent than I, so send him with me as a helper to support what I say, for I truly fear they may reject me” (28:34). Two brothers — two temperaments, two sets of gifts; a stammerer's zeal matched to a fluent tongue. The sūrah's presentation of mercy matures to this point: God's care stoops to the smallest organizational features of the mission — the personnel plan, the communications chart of a prophet's household. No court of heaven scorns staffing problems.

**A Prophet Alongside, Standing in the Signs**

The chapter ranks Aaron carefully: a prophet, but not given an independent scripture — his office is the support of the one revelation brought through Moses.
